So it’s a directional derivative that states how a function of multiple variables changes with space. So this is never with time, this is always with space. It operates on a scalar field, a function f that can be of x, y, z. Another example is conservative forces. These are also forces that you’ve seen in your module 2 in the physics course. a conservative force can be obtained by taking the derivative of the potential energy function. So if you think of gravitational potential energy, elastic potential energy, the force by gravity or by a spring is then minus the spatial derivative of this potential function. So if there’s a region where the potential changes rapidly, there’s large forces at play. And if F is in a positive direction, then if you push a particle or a point mass, in that direction, you will decrease its potential energy. Again, for physics, there’s always a minus sign involved. For mathematicians, it doesn’t matter, plus or minus. It’s just a sign convention. For physics, it does matter. It’s always towards lower concentration or towards lower potential. Yeah, so that’s what a conservative force do. It pushes the system towards lower potential energy. So how does it work? If you think of spring energy, half kx squared, if X describes the displacement from the nominal or equilibrium position, then the potential energy is half kx squared, its derivative, or minus its derivative is minus kx, which is exactly the spring force. If you push towards negative x, you get a positive force pushing to the right, trying to push the mass back to its equilibrium position, and if you extend the mass to that direction, then it tries to pull back the mass to its equilibrium position. Same holds for potential energy, so gravitational potential energy is MGY, which is positive, the higher you go, the more potential energy you have. Minus the derivative is minus MG, which is a force always pointing downwards. So this is a constant slope, so a linear relation, which gives a constant force if you are close enough to Earth, then G is constant. The idea was very simple in a way, so what you did was you have an area in the XY plane, a domain, and you want to integrate a certain function of two variables on the domain that is depicted here by the graph here in green and then what you want to know is what is the volume under that surface and the idea is to do this by approximation split this up in very small squares for every square you take the corresponding function value that is an approximation of the height of such a bar then you can compute this volume and you add up all these volumes to get the integral and if you then make this division into small rectangles fine enough, you get a reasonable approximation and this approximation is described as the integral, the double integral over domain D of a function of two variables FXY and then we write DA for this small parts of the small area where you take a small step in both the X and the Y direction. Now, if you do so, you get several interesting properties. If your function is always positive, the integral should also be positive because you always have this function value is positive, also it can, of course, sometimes be negative, then you count that volume and it gives a negative contribution. If you integrate the one function, you just get the area of your domain. So also areas can be computed in this way simply by integrating the constant function equal to 1. And these were the more interesting properties that you also know for integrals in the one-dimensional case of linearity. And you see indeed out of the point of an M times N with an N times D matrix that exists, and it’s an M times D matrix, and that’s exactly this one. But in most cases where you have to do it also earlier, then you could use these chain rules much more easy and then you do not have to do all this technical things. But in the more channel situation, you would get this. And then it looks very much like the normal chain rule. You have a question? It’s a good question if the Jacobian is an invertible matrix. That’s very often in the condition. So we will use it quite a lot, and this Jacobian, it plays a wall in a section that I skip, 13.8 from the book, and that’s about implicit function theorem, that you have an description like this. I will continue in chapter 13 of the book, mainly about differentiation of functions of several variables. And the first subject that I want to do, and that will come back also later on in the lecture, is the chain rule. And you know the chain rule from calculus for functions of one variable. Then you had a function, let’s say h of x, and that was the composition of two functions, the f of the g of x. And if you then wanted to differentiate the function h, then you know you had the chain rule, the derivative of h was the derivative of f applied in gx times the derivative of g in x. What we want to do is to generalize this to a situation where you have functions of several variables. We were looking at curves in two or three-dimensional space, and we defined a vector depending on a parameter t, and you can think about this as time, but it not necessarily has to be that. But r is a mapping from an interval from a to b to, let’s say, R3, and it is of the form that at a certain value of t in that interval, you have a function x of t times the first unit vector plus y of t times the second unit vector plus z of t times the third unit vector. And we assume here that this function r of t is continuously differentiable. So, we assume that the function is continuous, otherwise you would get maybe single isolated points, and we want to have really a curve. So, if you change the t a little bit, you only move a little bit on the curve. That’s the idea, so it has to be continuous. It has to be differentiable, and the derivative has to be continuous. Now, such an, this is called an arc, and such an arc is non-singular if for all t in a, b, I assume that the velocity vector is non-zero. It’s the problem that it is zero, not that much, but in points where the velocity vector is zero, strange things can happen. So, for example, things like this, that you have a curve and you go on like this, and at this point the velocity gets the zero vector, and then once in a sudden you go like that. Now, this is possible, but, and it’s also still a curve, but you don’t call it an arc, because there you want to have that everything is nicely continuously differentiable. Here you get a strange thing, because you move here from a zero velocity and then exactly in the opposite direction, and that’s a little bit strange. This is still a contour, because we just say that contour or path is just a finite union of arcs that fit to each other, such that the end point of arc, let’s say with number i, is the begin point of the next one, arc i plus 1.
